d'ancrage A / B, terrain (stations), `h_rider` · **Verdict Gate** = régime, both terminations,
`DAF`.

**« régime choisi » struck from the Solve Gate** is what makes the verdict-less first run
coherent. As originally written, the first run blocked the engine and showed an empty chart,
defeating this section's own reason for inventing manual-mode ground (« garde au sol is live
from first launch »). Confirmed from the other side: the two numbers the app exists to produce
are both live without a regime — garde min is geometric fact and the tension envelope is
physics; all a regime adds is a divisor.

The rejected fourth « rider-free » gate, the rule that a failed Clearance Gate leaves the strip
a scrub track, and that rule's one geometric exception are all in that same section.

Below the gates, the split of responsibility is unchanged and has **one source of truth each**:

```
SOLVE / CLEARANCE / VERDICT GATES   (no physics — the conventions forbid re-deriving it)
      ↓ pass
ENGINE  (owns every physical rule)
  invalid-input{ field, value, bound, boundKind }   ← §5.4 chord bound
  no-convergence                                    ← bracket failed (modes Flèche & Tension)
  slack-limit                                       ← line lying down
      ↓
RESULT-LEVEL WARNINGS  (need a solve)
  > 30 % MBL (§9.2) · flèche < 3 % de la portée (§8.2) · `k` hors bande · verdict (§10)
```

The hard bound `L₀ > chord/(1 + T_break/EA)` needs `EA` and `MBF`, so evaluating it in the form
would re-derive physics in the UI. It stays in the engine — which **obliges `solveSpan`'s
pre-check to carry the §5.4 chord bound and report it as `invalid-input{field:"L0"}`** with its
violated bound (« voir § 2 — le contrat du moteur »).

**Two closing claims of ticket 04 §7 are struck. Do not restore them.**

- ~~« The chord bound is only user-violable in direct-`L₀` mode. »~~ **Struck**, measured false:
  the §5.4 floor **is** the breaking load — floor `L₀ = 197,404 m` on a 200 m level span yields
  `T_max = 100,0 % MBF`, and 987,02 m on a 1000 m span yields 101,1 % — so an over-`MBF` target
  maps through `resolveL0` to a sub-floor `L₀` that the same pre-check refuses. The bound is
  violable in **all three** tightness modes.
- ~~« `H_target ≥ MBF` is not an input error: it solves perfectly and the verdict says
  rupture. »~~ **Struck**, for the same reason. **No user-facing rupture verdict is reachable
  that way.** The state this surfaces as is the refusal « Tension impossible », which carries
  the attainable bound in the driving field's own quantity and unit, and no Fix Action — because
  the bound *is* a rupture design and no unambiguous repair exists.

The empty-required-field state is **neutral, lowercase, no icon** — « valeur requise » — and it
states no global consequence, because the consequence of an empty field is rendered on the
rail's output half, not on the control. Committed-invalid is **⛔ and blocking**. Calling both
« erreur » would make ordinary typing feel like a fault, which is the point of the split. One
copy detail: ticket 04's amendment wrote this message as « Valeur requise. » and ticket 11's
message table as « valeur requise »; rendering is ticket 11's, so **the lowercase, unpunctuated
form governs**. Both message columns in full — « voir § 7 — validation et états d'erreur ».

### Champ responsable — attribution, never inference

**Invariant: no hard check ever renders only on a read-out.** Every check declared above is
declared on a *quantity* and attributed to an editable field by the static table keyed by
(quantity × mode) in « voir § 7 — validation et états d'erreur » — never by inference. The
invariant exists because two of the three tightness modes make `L₀` a read-out and imported mode
makes `L` and `h` read-outs: `station B = station A` yields `L = 0`, reaches `solveSpan`, and
returns `invalid-input{field:"L"}` pointing at a field the user cannot edit.

Two rules of that table are fixed *here*, because they are properties of §13.1 rather than of
the error surface. **An overridden derived value is its own culprit** — override turns the
read-out into an editable field, so attribution collapses to identity. And **the steel rows
follow the formulas, not the field group**: `MBF = K·d²·R₀/1000` depends on all three inputs,
while `EA = E_app·C·d²` and `μ = W·d²/100` depend on **`d` alone**, since `E_app`, `C` and `W`
are pinned midpoints and not construction-indexed. Listing Grade and Construction as culprits
for `EA` would send a user to two fields that cannot move the number.


---

## § 4 — Terrain et import de profil CSV

The ground under the line. One concept, two sources — a straight line between the two anchor
bases (manual), or a profile read from an imported **Trace** — and the simulation never
distinguishes them: the straight line is the two-point case of the same `Terrain` value
(`CONTEXT.md`, ticket 04 §1). This section specifies the imported source: the file contract, the
Trace→`Terrain` derivation, anchor placement on the Trace, the shared x domain, the degenerate
placements and the parse-failure taxonomy.

Source: [ticket 06 — CSV profile import and anchor
placement](./issues/06-csv-profile-import-and-anchor-placement.md) and [ticket 16 — Profile CSV
export, can it exceed 200 points?](./issues/16-profile-export-point-count.md), with
[ticket 01](./issues/01-gpx-parsing-in-the-browser.md) **superseded**.

**The only terrain file format in v1 is the profile CSV** — the Swiss federal geoportal profile
export (`map.geo.admin.ch` → outil Profil → export CSV), `Distance;Altitude` plus optional
coordinates. **GPX import is out of scope** (voir § 1 — périmètre) and ticket 01's GPX-parsing
research is **superseded**: nothing in it is inherited, no `DOMParser` mapper, no `<ele>`
policy, no haversine question. The two formats differ in kind, not syntax — a GPX track is
*recorded* (noisy, irregularly spaced, curved, elevation optional per point) where a profile CSV
is *drawn and DEM-sampled* (straight, monotonic, altitude always present) — so a GPX path would
resurrect the resampling, smoothing and chord-projection questions the CSV pivot **deleted**.

There are **no network calls at runtime**: no DEM fallback, no map view, no reverse geocoding.
The file the operator brings is the only terrain data that exists.

### Ce que le format est — mesuré, pas supposé

Three real traces live in `docs/references/`. **Three, not two** — tickets 06, 09 and 14 each
say "two"; `example-profile-trace3.csv` is the third and it is a third independent corroboration
of the 200-point cap at a third distinct length.

| Propriété | trace1 | trace2 | trace3 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Lignes (hors en-tête) | **200** | **200** | **200** |
| Étendue `Distance` | 0 → 183,4 m | 0 → 224,0 m | 0 → 188,4 m |
| Pas (min / max) | 0,90 / 1,00 m | 1,10 / 1,20 m | ≈0,95 m |
| `Distance` strictement croissante | oui | oui | oui |
| Cellules `Altitude` manquantes | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| Corde planimétrique `Easting`/`Northing` | 183,42 m | 223,96 m | — |
| `Distance` ÷ corde | **0,99991** | **1,00018** | — |
| Écart latéral max à la corde | **0,007 m** | **0,009 m** | — |
| Longueur 3-D le long du terrain | **263,9 m** | — | — |
| Encodage | ASCII, LF, sans BOM, `;`, décimale `.` | idem | idem |

Header, verbatim:

```
Distance;Altitude;Easting;Northing;Longitude;Latitude
0;540.4;2547727.43;1192457.59;6.752859;46.881175
0.9;540.4;2547727.06;1192456.74;6.752854;46.881167
```

Four findings from that table are load-bearing and each one **closed** a question rather than
opening one: `Distance` is horizontal, the trace is straight by construction (7–9 mm), the
exporter emits a fixed *count* not a fixed *pitch*, and every row carries an altitude — so there
is no partial-elevation case to design, only a row-level parse policy.

### Le contrat de fichier — colonnes par nom, minimum `Distance` + `Altitude`

Parse the header row and locate columns **by name, case-insensitively**. Require `Distance` and
`Altitude`. Ignore every other column except `Easting`/`Northing` (or `Longitude`/`Latitude`),
consumed only by the straightness check. **Auto-detect the delimiter (`;` or `,`) and the
decimal mark (`.` or `,`) from the file** — `;` *is* the European-Excel convention, so any Excel
round-trip on a fr-CH machine rewrites `540.4` as `540,4` and a legitimate export must survive
it.

```
Distance;Altitude;Easting;Northing;Longitude;Latitude   ✓ swisstopo, verbatim
distance;altitude                                       ✓ minimum fait main
Distance;Altitude    (décimales « 540,4 »)              ✓ aller-retour Excel fr-CH
Altitude;Distance                                       ✓ par nom, donc ordre libre
0;540.4                                                 ✗ pas d'en-tête → rejeté
D;Z                                                     ✗ non reconnu → rejeté
```

**No positional fallback.** That is the whole point: a file whose first two columns are
`Altitude;Distance` parses "successfully" under a positional reader and yields ground that is
wrong in a way **no downstream check can catch** — the altitudes become stations and the
clearance read-out is confidently false. Matching by name makes the swapped-column class
*unrepresentable*. The price is rejecting a headerless two-column file; that is worth paying,
and the rejection message says exactly what is missing (voir § 7 — validation et états d'erreur,
`colonnes-manquantes`, which names the columns it *did* read).

*Rejected: the strict swisstopo shape only* (exact 6-column header, `;`, `.`) rather than
columns-by-name, because maximum provenance certainty refuses both a hand-made two-column file
and any Excel round-trip of a legitimate export — a lot of friction to buy a guarantee the app
never uses. The permissiveness also has a designed side effect: it makes a **hand-authored or
survey-derived two-column CSV a first-class input**, which is the only escape hatch that exists
for an operator who needs terrain finer than the geoportal's cap.

### `Distance` est horizontale — la mesure qui a tranché

`docs/simulation-theory.md` §2 fixes the frame: « Left anchor `A = (0, 0)`; right anchor `B =
(L, h)`; `x` horizontal toward B, `z` up. `h < 0` means B lower than A. » So `x` is
**horizontal**, and the file's `Distance` must be read the same way. It is:

| Lecture | Valeur | Écart |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Distance` rapportée (trace1) | 183,4 m | — |
| Corde planimétrique `Easting`/`Northing` | 183,42 m | **2×10⁻⁴** |
| Longueur de surface 3-D le long du terrain | 263,9 m | **+44 %** |

Reading `Distance` as *surface* distance would inflate every `L` by nearly half on broken ground
— silently and plausibly, since 263,9 is a perfectly believable number for the same drawn line.
The horizontal reading is confirmed to 2×10⁻⁴ on trace1 and 1,8×10⁻⁴ on trace2.

Three consequences, all of them "do not add this":

- **No 3-D or slope correction anywhere.** `d[i]` is `x`, after the origin shift of the next
  section. Any pass that "corrects" `Distance` for slope re-introduces the 44 % error.
- **No cumulative-haversine and no projection onto the A→B chord.** The file hands over
  along-line horizontal distance directly and the line *is* the chord (7–9 mm lateral). Both
  candidate reductions are dead.
- **A different tool's `Distance` semantics are not assumed, they are contracted.** Ticket 16
  recorded the alternative-denser-route question as *unexamined*, not as a negative finding; the
  app's guard is the columns-by-name contract plus the straightness disclosure, never trust.

### Résolution — les échantillons du fichier, jamais de rééchantillonnage

`Terrain` keeps **exactly the points the file gave**. Ticket 03's `xs`/`zs` carry no uniformity
requirement and `zGroundAt` already interpolates linearly, so non-uniform input costs nothing
(voir § 2 — le contrat du moteur).

The exporter emits a **fixed 200 points, not a fixed pitch**, so spacing is `length/199` and it
degrades precisely where spans get long enough to matter:

| Portée | Pas exporté |
| --- | --- |
| 183 m | 0,92 m |
| 500 m | 2,5 m |
| **≈995 m** | **5,0 m — seuil de l'avertissement** |
| 1500 m | 7,5 m |
| 2000 m | 10,0 m |

**200 is the ceiling, per export and not per line.** Provenance: **operator-attested, not
documentation-verified** (ticket 16, answered directly by the user 2026-08-12) — recorded
plainly, and corroborated from the other side by measurement, since three traces of three
different lengths all landing on *exactly* 200 rows is the signature of a fixed sample count,
not of a default that went unchanged three times. **No re-export helps**, which is why the
warning copy states a limitation instead of offering a remedy: an operator told only
« résolution grossière » re-exports and gets the same 200 rows.

The arithmetic consequence the spec must carry: since the ~5 m threshold is crossed at
**≈995 m**, **coarse terrain is the *normal* state on the upper half of the map's 50–2000 m
range**, not an exceptional one. And this frequency claim is **arithmetic, not observed** — all
three repo traces are ≤ 224 m, so nobody has ever seen a coarse file. State it that way or the
claim reads as field experience. The warning nevertheless stays at the ⚠ tier inside the
always-visible terrain block (voir § 6 — sorties, verdict et provenance): it is conditional on a
design choice the user controls, and it names a **blind spot in garde au sol**, the app's
primary output.

**Resampling to a uniform grid is rejected**, and it is the obvious-looking fix. Linear
interpolation between two DEM samples 10 m apart adds **zero** terrain knowledge while making
the array *look* ten times more resolved: a bump between two samples stays exactly as invisible
as before, and a coarse import becomes indistinguishable from a fine one. For a clearance tool
that is the non-conservative direction — it manufactures confidence. Same argument, same
verdict, as dropping malformed rows (§ Échecs de parsing below). The honest move is
**disclosure**: the measured max spacing is always shown, warning or not.

Two places where resampling would come back through a side door, both already closed:

- **The URL payload.** Ticket 09's `p1.` trace encoding is explicitly forbidden from shrinking
  itself by resampling — « shrinking the payload by resampling would smuggle it back in through
  the side door ». It compresses by delta + zigzag varint at cm resolution instead, altitude
  quantised by **ceiling** so the single lossy step is provably safe-side (voir § 8 — état, URL
  et coquille applicative).
- **The clearance scan.** See immediately below.

**Vertex-only clearance evaluation is safe — nobody should densify that scan.**
`docs/simulation-theory.md` §11 requires the inner minimum over *all* `x` — `h_min = min over
x_P of min over x of h_clear(x; x_P)`, with `h_clear(x; x_P) = z_cable(x; x_P) − z_ground(x) −
h_rider` and `h_rider` default 2.1 m `[CONVENTION]` — and ticket 03 has `evaluateClearance` scan
at the terrain's own sample points. In principle the minimum can hide *between* two samples:
`z_cable` is convex, `z_ground` is piecewise linear, so `h_clear` is convex on each terrain
segment and a convex function's interior minimum is legal. The magnitude of the miss is the
cable's own sag across one segment — at a 1500 m span with 50 m sag, across a 7,5 m segment,
**≈5 mm**. Vertex evaluation therefore stands. Densifying the scan would spend ticket 12's
measured sweep budget (p95 1,30 ms at 240 steps against the pre-agreed 8 ms) to recover
millimetres, and the clearance reducer is the larger half of that budget.

Also rejected: **hard-rejecting a coarse file**. It would block the long-span case outright —
which, per the arithmetic above, is the upper half of the supported range.

**Deliberately unchased, recorded so nobody re-opens it believing a decision hangs on it:** the
DEM's own native resolution is unknown. It would sharpen the *explanation* (at 1500 m, is 7,5 m
discarding data the terrain model holds, or already at the raster's floor?) but changes no
behaviour, because the app is designed never to need to know what the source could have
provided.

> **ADR candidate** (voir § 12 — décisions à consigner) — *use the file's own samples and
> disclose the spacing* over *resample to a uniform grid*. Hard to reverse, surprising without
> context, and the kind of choice a later "quality" sweep fixes without knowing it was
> deliberate.

### Rectitude — vérifiée quand les coordonnées sont là

The format permits exactly **one** modelling error and this catches it: nothing stops a user
drawing a **bent polyline** on the geoportal. Then `Distance` runs along the bend while the zip
line is the straight chord between anchors — `L` comes out too long, the terrain gets stretched,
and without the check the app cannot tell.

The metric is **one pass over `Easting`/`Northing`** (or `Longitude`/`Latitude`): max lateral
deviation from the A→B chord, plus the `Distance` ÷ chord ratio. Three states, non-blocking, and
explicitly **not** a rejection — a slight bend to route around an obstacle is real surveying
practice, and blocking it would leave no way to proceed: **✓ rectiligne** with the measured max
deviation, **⚠ non rectiligne** naming the deviation and stating that `L` is therefore
overestimated, and **ℹ rectitude non vérifiable** when the coordinates are absent. Their exact
copy is in « voir § 6 — sorties, verdict et provenance », where they render.

The third state is not a warning about a value, it is a warning about the **absence of a
check**, and it must not read like the second: « we could not check » and « we checked and it is
bad » are different things. It is also exactly where the denser-terrain escape hatch lands — a
hand-authored two-column CSV has no coordinates — so the operator who buys clearance fidelity
from another source pays for it with the straightness check, and the import documentation says
so in one sentence.

Straightness travels in a shared link as the scalar `dev` (cm, with a sentinel for « non
vérifiable »); `Easting`/`Northing` are not embedded, because they were only ever consumed by a
check already performed at send time (voir § 8 — état, URL et coquille applicative).

### La Trace est immuable ; le `Terrain` est dérivé à un seul joint

The imported profile is held **once, immutably, in file coordinates**. The engine's `Terrain` is
*derived* where it is needed — one seam, one translation.

```ts
// Session state — file coordinates, never mutated.
type Trace = {
  d: Float64Array;          // Distance, m, horizontal, strictly increasing — from the file
  z: Float64Array;          // Altitude, m
  dev: number | null;       // max lateral deviation from the A→B chord, m; null = unverifiable
  fileName: string | null;  // null ⇒ « tracé reçu par lien » (ticket 09 drops the filename)
};

type AnchorPlacement = { station: number; height: number };   // Station, Anchor Height

// The one seam. Pure. No mutation of `trace`.
function deriveTerrain(trace: Trace, a: AnchorPlacement, b: AnchorPlacement): Terrain;
```

```
state:   trace    = { d, z, dev, fileName }        // file coords, never mutated
         anchorA  = { station: 62.0,  height: 4.0 }
         anchorB  = { station: 245.0, height: 2.5 }

derived: L = anchorB.station − anchorA.station
         terrain = { kind: "profile",
                     xs: [0, …d[i] − 62 for 62 < d[i] < 245…, L],
                     zs: [interp(62), …,                      interp(245)] }
```

Three steps, in this order: **subtract `d_A`**, **slice to `[0, L]`**, **insert interpolated
endpoints at exactly `x = 0` and `x = L`**. The endpoint insertion is not tidiness: `d_A` will
generally land between two samples, and without synthesised endpoints `zGroundAt` would be asked
to **extrapolate** at the span's ends — which is where the anchors are and where the clearance
read-outs get the most scrutiny. Post-condition: `xs[0] === 0` and `xs[xs.length − 1] === L`
exactly.

Moving an anchor is a **different derivation, never a mutation** of imported data. Two
consequences fall out for free: what gets persisted is the file, not a view of it, and
re-anchoring is always possible because the original is never lost. Ticket 03's `Terrain` union
needs **no change**.

*Rejected: translating and slicing at import time.* Every anchor drag would re-derive the stored
array, and the original has to be kept anyway for provenance and re-anchoring — so nothing is
saved. *Rejected: extending `Terrain` with an `x0` offset field.* It changes a type ticket 03
fixed and puts an offset in the clearance inner loop.

### Placement des ancrages — quatre champs et deux poignées

`Portée` and `Dénivelé` are **typed directly in manual mode and derived read-outs in imported
mode** — two framings deliberately (ticket 04 §1), because `solveSpan` wants exactly `(L, h)`
and forcing the anchor-placement framing onto manual mode would make every metre of dénivelé
come from tower height.

```
Station A  [  62,0 ] m     Hauteur d'ancrage A  [ 4,0 ] m
Station B  [ 245,0 ] m     Hauteur d'ancrage B  [ 2,5 ] m

Valeurs déduites
  Portée L      183,0 m
  Dénivelé h    −38,6 m
  Alt. base A   540,4 m   ·   Alt. base B  501,8 m
```

```
L = d_B − d_A
h = (z(d_B) + hauteur_B) − (z(d_A) + hauteur_A)
```

`z(d)` is the linear interpolation of the profile at station `d`. The two `hauteur d'ancrage`
fields are **the ones ticket 04 already created** for manual mode — no new field concept — and
they keep their meaning: they feed the `Terrain` and the anchor altitudes, **never
`solveSpan`**, which takes span and elevation difference measured anchor to anchor. All four
fields are `<input type="text" inputMode="decimal">` displaying the comma, like every numeric
field in the app (voir § 3 — le modèle de paramètres).

**Two draggable handles**, reusing ticket 07's rule verbatim: **pointer input snaps to a terrain
sample, a typed station is exact and interpolates.** The justification transfers intact — 1 px ≈
1,2 m at 1500 m, so there is no precision in a drag to preserve, while a station taken from a
site survey must be enterable to the metre.

The handles do **not** sit on the profile. They live in a dedicated **24 px Handle Lane**
between the plot area and the x-axis labels, reserved at every width and in both terrain modes —
in manual mode it draws the non-interactive span bracket (voir § 5 — visualisation et mise en
page). The collision that forces this is structural, not a sizing accident: the anchors are
pinned at the domain edges, so a 44 px target at 390 px covers the first and last 12 % of the
span and overlaps the rider glyph. Reserving the lane in manual mode too means the profile's
height never changes on import.

*Rejected: fields only* — placing an anchor on a visible terrain feature becomes trial and error
against the chart. *Rejected: chart clicks only* — no way to type an exact station, which is how
a real span gets specified.

### Deux sources, un concept — ce qui survit à un import et à un retrait

```
manuel                    import                     retrait
L  183,0  (saisi)   ─►   L  183,4  (déduit)   ─►   L  183,0  (saisi)
h  −30,0  (saisi)   ─►   h  −38,6  (déduit)   ─►   h  −30,0  (saisi)
haut. A  4,0        ─►   haut. A  4,0         ─►   haut. A  4,0
haut. B  2,5        ─►   haut. B  2,5         ─►   haut. B  2,5
                         stations 0 → 183,4        stations abandonnées,
                                                   tracé gardé en mémoire
```

**On import, anchor A goes to the first station and B to the last** — the trace *is* the
intended span. These files are drawn *for the line*: the operator opened the geoportal, drew the
span, exported it. So the import yields a complete, solvable geometry with zero further input.

**Manual `L`/`h` stay in state untouched and are restored exactly when the trace is removed.**
This mirrors ticket 05's retain-across-family-flip rule and ticket 04's finding that discarding
a user's parallel input is worth a blocking error. The `hauteur d'ancrage` fields need no
special handling in either direction: they describe the installation, not the terrain.

**Removal discards the stations, not the Trace.** The profile stays cached in the Session, and
the file-identity line in `Paramètres › Géométrie` has **four** states:

| État | Copie | Action |
| --- | --- | --- |
| aucun tracé | *(absent)* | — |
| tracé actif, importé | « *nom.csv* » | `[ Retirer le tracé ]` |
| tracé actif, reçu par lien | « tracé reçu par lien » | `[ Retirer le tracé ]` |
| tracé en mémoire, inactif | « Tracé en mémoire : *nom.csv* (non utilisé) » | **`[ Réutiliser ce tracé ]`** |

For a link-received trace the fourth line reads « tracé reçu par lien (non utilisé) », since the
filename does not travel. `[ Réutiliser ce tracé ]` re-places A at the first station and B at
the last, exactly as an import does. Without this path « Réinitialiser le scénario »'s promise
to keep the Trace cached is empty and the reset is destructive in effect. « Effacer la session »
is the one control that deletes the cached Trace, behind a confirmation, with an extra warning
when the Trace arrived by link and therefore cannot be re-imported (voir § 8 — état, URL et
coquille applicative).

`Paramètres › Géométrie` owns three controls no earlier field table listed: **« Importer un
profil… » is a button, not a drop zone** (the chart already carries three drag targets),
« Retirer le tracé » (enabled only with a trace loaded), and the file-identity line above. Parse
failures render inline beneath them.

*Rejected: adopting the existing `L`* (place A at 0, B at station `L`). It preserves `L` but `h`
changes anyway because the terrain decides it — half-preserved, harder to explain, and it needs
a further fallback rule whenever the trace is shorter than `L`. *Rejected: overwrite and reset*,
which makes an accidental import destroy typed geometry with no undo.

### Domaine x partagé — portée + 5 %, borné à l'étendue du tracé

```
  marge │        portée [0, L]        │ marge
  ┈┈┈┈┈┈┼──A──────────────────────B──┼┈┈┈┈┈┈   profil
        │   ╲__________________/     │
  ──────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────   garde au sol
 (vide) │  ╲───────────────────────╱  │ (vide)
        0                             L
```

**Domain = `[d_A − 0,05 L, d_B + 0,05 L]`, clamped to the trace extent** — so a whole-trace
import makes the domain exactly the trace. Both charts consume **one** `SharedX` object with
**one** `GUTTER = 24` and one pointer inverse (voir § 5 — visualisation et mise en page).

**This is constrained, not chosen.** The clearance-vs-position curve doubles as the scrub track
and is aligned under the profile, which only works if both charts share an x domain — and the
clearance curve's domain is rider position, i.e. `[0, L]`. « Show the whole trace in the profile
chart » is therefore not a free option: it would silently break the mechanism the whole layout
exists for, putting the marker under the cursor at a different `x` in each chart.

The 5 % margin resolves the counter-pressure. With a domain of exactly `[0, L]`, dragging anchor
A inward rescales the chart under the cursor *and* makes the terrain you just excluded vanish,
so you cannot see what you are dragging away from. Because the domain is shared, the clearance
curve is simply **undefined outside `[0, L]`** — which marks the span's boundaries visually for
free rather than needing a separate cue. Horizontal rescale while dragging an anchor is
consistent with the per-render vertical auto-fit.

Note for the implementer: this is the domain the vertical-exaggeration ceiling of **×1 … ×5,5**
was published against (`VE = plot aspect × (x-domain span / z-domain span)`), and it is why the
earlier ×4,7 figure is ~10 % low — the domain was widened after that measurement.

### Placements dégénérés — bloquer, avec une réparation en un clic

**A structural finding first, because it relocates validation.** Ticket 04 attached every hard
range to a **field** (`L > 0` finite, `h` finite). In imported mode `L` and `h` are *derived
read-outs*, so those checks have nothing to hang off. They must still run — on the derived
values, with the error **attributed back to whichever station field can fix it**, via the static
culprit table keyed by (quantity × mode): `L` importé → Station A/B, `h` importé → Stations
+ hauteurs (voir § 7 — validation et états d'erreur). Otherwise `station B = station A` yields
`L = 0`, reaches `solveSpan`, and returns `invalid-input{field:"L"}` pointing at a field the
user cannot edit. The governing invariant: **no hard check ever renders only on a read-out.**

| Cas | Comportement |
| --- | --- |
| `station B ≤ station A` | ⛔ « L doit être > 0 — l'ancrage B est avant l'ancrage A. » `[ Inverser A et B ]` |
| station saisie 260 sur un tracé de 224 m | ⛔ « Station hors du tracé (0 – 224,0 m). » |
| poignée glissée au-delà de l'extrémité | bornée à l'extrémité du tracé — l'état d'erreur est inatteignable |
| tracé de 40 m ⇒ `L = 40` | ⚠ « Hors du domaine de validité (50 – 2000 m) » *(avertissement ticket 04, non bloquant)* |

**`[ Inverser A et B ]` rather than a silent auto-swap**, because auto-normalising to `A =
min(station)` would **flip the sign of `h`** — exactly the silent model reinterpretation the
§10.4 family gate ruled out. The one-click fix earns its place because the repair is mechanical
and unambiguous; making the user retype both stations is friction with no safety benefit. Per
the error contract, the *message* never asserts an act while the *button label* describes its
effect — both must read correctly on the first frame of an arriving link, where nothing was
"entered" at all.

**Typed stations block; dragged handles clamp.** Same asymmetry as typed-is-exact /
pointer-is-constrained, so the two input paths stay consistent across the app. Clamping produces
no error state, so this asymmetry needs no machinery in the error model — it lives entirely in
the input handler. The one deliberate exception is `x_D`, the rider's viewing position, which
**clamps on every path** (including typed and link arrival) and says so with a transient inline
note, because it selects a view rather than a design input.

### Échecs de parsing — rejeter le fichier entier, nommer la ligne

Seven blocking kinds. Every message is **prefixed with the file name** (the operator may have
picked the wrong file of several), they render **inline under the file control** in `Paramètres
› Géométrie` with `[ Choisir un autre fichier ]`, and **never in the banner slot** — nothing
about the current line changed.

The seven kinds are `colonnes-manquantes` · `aucune-donnée` · `trop-peu-de-points` ·
`valeur-non-numérique` · `distance-non-croissante` · `distance-dupliquée` ·
`lecture-impossible`; their exact French copy, file-name prefix included, is tabulated once in
« voir § 7 — validation et états d'erreur ».

This taxonomy **replaces** ticket 01's GPX taxonomy (`not-xml` / `not-gpx` / `empty-track` /
`missing-elevation`), superseded with the rest of that research.

**Any bad row rejects the whole file, with its line number.** A skipped row is a lost terrain
bump, so importing-what-parses manufactures a smoother profile than the data supports — the same
non-conservative move as resampling, and a warning count does not say *where* the gap is. These
files come from a machine exporter, so a malformed row means hand-editing or a different tool:
worth stopping for, and the line number makes stopping actionable.

**A rejected file leaves the current terrain untouched** — manual geometry or a previously
loaded trace both survive. Destroying it would turn a rejected import into data loss.

`distance-dupliquée` has a format-induced cause worth recording: spacing is `length/199` and
`Distance` is written to 0,1 m, so **any trace under ~20 m produces duplicate `Distance`
values** — two altitudes at one `x`, i.e. ambiguous ground. That is far below the 50 m validity
floor, so rejecting costs nothing real.

One non-blocking hint, and it belongs to the **terrain provenance block** rather than here,
because that file *was* accepted and the hint qualifies terrain now in use:

```
⚠ Distances peut-être en km plutôt qu'en mètres ?
```

Fires when the total distance is < 5 m — the km-vs-m confusion, otherwise indistinguishable from
a legitimately tiny trace. Recorded because it will otherwise read as dead code: it is reachable
**only for hand-authored files**, since the sub-20 m duplicate-`Distance` rule rejects any
geoportal trace short enough to trigger it as `distance-dupliquée` first. It is kept for exactly
that case, which is the denser-terrain escape hatch.

### Ce que le terrain publie — renvoi

The terrain block is **always present, in manual mode too**, in its own sub-block of the rail's
`Provenance` section, above the aggregate list, and the point count and max spacing are shown
**whether or not a warning fires** — otherwise the absence of a warning is ambiguous, and by the
same argument the absence of the *block* would be ambiguous in manual mode. The rendered copy
for all four cases — a fine import, a coarse import, the three straightness states, and manual
ground carrying the measured « le dénivelé ne gagne aucune garde » consequence — is written
once, in « voir § 6 — sorties, verdict et provenance ».

### Faits mesurés à ne pas re-litiger

Condensed, because each one is a decision an implementer would otherwise re-open:

| Fait | Conséquence |
| --- | --- |
| `Distance` horizontale (183,4 vs corde 183,42 vs surface 263,9 m) | aucune correction 3-D ; sinon `L` gonflé de 44 % |
| Tracés rectilignes à 7–9 mm — lignes *dessinées*, DEM-échantillonnées | ni haversine, ni projection sur la corde, ni lissage |
| 200 points fixes, pas `length/199`, plafond **par export** | terrain grossier au-delà de ≈995 m, ré-export inutile |
| Trois tracés d'exemple : 183,4 / 224,0 / 188,4 m, 200 lignes chacun | troisième corroboration du plafond |
| Manque de convexité ≈5 mm à 1500 m | évaluation aux sommets seulement ; ne pas densifier le balayage |
| Rectitude = une passe sur `Easting`/`Northing` | seule erreur de modélisation que le format permet |
| Trace ≈10,4 KB / 200 lignes, `p1.` mesuré à 910 / 859 / 897 caractères | le tracé voyage dans l'URL, sans rééchantillonnage |
| Résolution native du MNT inconnue | change l'explication, pas le comportement |


---

## § 5 — Visualisation and layout — the Chart Block

Everything the user *looks at* lives in one region, the **Chart Block**. This section fixes what
draws it, what it contains, how the rider is moved, how a clearance failure is signalled, and
how the whole block reflows from a 1440 px desktop to a 390 px phone. Provenance:
[ticket 02](./issues/02-profile-chart-rendering-approach.md) (renderer and the exaggeration
policy), [ticket 07](./issues/07-profile-visualisation-prototype.md) (the spine, the input paths,
the layers, the failure signals) **with its three 2026-08-12 amendments**, and
[ticket 15](./issues/15-responsive-and-narrow-screen-layout.md) (the layout contract, which is
where the buildable numbers are and where two defects in the prototype were found and fixed).

The prototype route is **not** the reference artifact and is not linked here: ticket 07 §12
schedules it for deletion and the PNGs it cites are not in the repo. **Ticket 07's prose is the
reference**, and its deletion is sequenced — voir § 10 — ordre d'implémentation.

### Renderer — hand-rolled SVG, `d3-shape`, one hand-written affine scale per axis

**Hand-rolled SVG in React**, with `d3-shape` (~5,7 KB gzip, no DOM coupling) generating the
line and area paths, and a **hand-written affine scale function per axis**. No charting library.
`d3-scale` is skipped too: the only transform needed is linear metres → pixels, and it costs 16
KB gzip with no bundled types.

Three reasons, in the order ticket 02 weighted them.

| Reason | Evidence |
| --- | --- |
| SSR is free — it is just JSX | Recharts' SSR story is unresolved and explicitly broken under streaming SSR (its own discussion #6390); Observable Plot needs an explicit JSDOM `document` wired in; uPlot's canvas model has no SSR path at all |
| Weight | ~6 KB against 127–147 KB for Observable Plot or Recharts |
| It matches the actual problem | Independent, **deliberately mismatched** x and y scales are the whole point. Two plain affine functions express that directly; a Cartesian-chart abstraction has to be fought into it |

Rejections to carry:

- **Hand-rolled SVG rather than Recharts / Observable Plot / uPlot / D3-driving-the-DOM, because
  every packaged option has a documented SSR wrinkle and none of them wants two mismatched
  scales.**
- **`d3-shape` rather than `d3-scale`, because the only transform is linear metres → pixels and
  `d3-scale` costs 16 KB gzip and ships no types.**
- **`visx` low-level primitives (`@visx/shape`, `@visx/group`, `@visx/drag`) are the recorded
  fallback** — SSR-safe, natively typed, React 19 compatible — which **lost narrowly on
  dependency parsimony**, since nothing it offers here is more than `d3-shape` plus JSX. If
  hand-rolled path and area logic becomes unwieldy with terrain, the clearance band and the
  handle lane all on screen, `visx` is the pre-approved move; it is not a new decision.

Ticket 13's route-level `ssr: false` does **not** re-open this. The other two reasons stand
alone, and the only chart markup the server emits is the Skeleton's two empty chart frames —
voir § 8 — état, URL et coquille applicative.

Surveyed and deliberately **not designed in v1**: SVG accessibility via `<title>`, and
client-side export via `XMLSerializer` (ticket 02 §5). The export is *not* ruled out by the
map's no-print-route ruling — it needs no route and carries no verdict — but v1 does not build
it.

### The spine — one Chart Block, five bands

Ticket 07 built four variants and let the measurements decide: A *chart plein cadre* with a HUD,
B *tableau de bord*, C *profil + courbe de garde*, and D the composite the grill converged on.
The winner is **C's two linked charts carrying B's fixed read-out strip**.

```
┌ RAIL (une colonne défilante, 288 px) ┬ CHART BLOCK ─────────────────────────────┐
│ en-tête du rail                      │ créneau bandeau                          │
│ Paramètres                           │ profil (badge · tracé · couloir · axe x) │
│ Valeurs déduites                     │ bande de garde au sol  = piste de scrub  │
│ Résultats                            │ VIEW STRIP                               │
│ Provenance                           │ 5 cellules de lecture                    │
│ Ce que cet outil ne calcule pas      │ (marge de boîtage, vide)                 │
└──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

| Band | Owns | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **créneau bandeau** (banner slot) | exactly one occupant, by precedence | not this section's — voir § 7 — validation et états d'erreur |
| **profil** | terrain, cable, anchors, rider, clearance dimension, VE badge, danger bands, **Handle Lane** | one SVG |
| **bande de garde au sol** (clearance strip) | garde au sol vs rider position — **and it *is* the scrub track** | one SVG, same x scale |
| **View Strip** | 2 layer toggles · « ×1 vraie échelle » · the numeric `x` field with its `%` secondary | 44 px, one touch-height row |
| **cellules** | garde · `T` ancrage A · `T` ancrage B · pente locale · flèche | 5 cells, **outside the plot area** |

The deciding argument for C's shape was **question 5 of ticket 07 — the failure case, not
taste**: the clearance curve crossing zero is the only presentation where the violation is
impossible to miss, because **a zero crossing is categorical** while a tinted band on a
vertically distorted profile is a judgement call. A and B both reduce the most important fact
the tool produces to a shading.

- **Read-outs in fixed cells outside the plot area rather than on the chart, because both
  alternatives died of occlusion**: A's HUD sits over anchor B — a corner overlay on a
  full-bleed chart will always cover part of the span, and the span is the subject — and C's
  follow-the-rider tooltip covers the cable it describes, which is exactly the « moving numbers
  next to a moving marker » failure ticket 07 set out to test.
- **Five cells, both anchor tensions, no `T max` and no `H`.** On an inclined span the governing
  end is not obvious, terminations are judged against `T_A` and `T_B` separately, and watching
  the two swap as the rider crosses is genuinely informative. `H` stays out: identical at both
  ends, and engine plumbing.
- **There is no "results panel."** The aggregates live in the Rail's output half; the cells
  carry Position-scope numbers only. The two scopes are labelled — voir § 6 — sorties, verdict
  et provenance.

The Chart Block element itself carries `container-type: inline-size` **and** the app's single
`ResizeObserver`. No extra wrapper: inline-size containment does not change the box's own inline
size (it is a definite-width flex child) and the observer still reports the same content box.
The observer needs **`inlineSize` only** — every band height is width-derived (voir the layout
contract below) — and its seed is **`null`**, rendering the Skeleton until the first
measurement.

- **Seed `null` rather than `{ width: 900, height: 420 }`, because the prototype's seed drew a
  900 px chart inside a 390 px box on the first client frame.** A `null` seed also makes ticket
  13's Skeleton dimensionally correct at 390 px for free.

### Layers — a fixed always-on set plus exactly two toggles

**Always on:** terrain (filled area), the loaded cable, both anchors, the rider marker with its
**2,1 m envelope**, the clearance dimension line, the VE badge, and the danger bands *whenever a
violation exists* — the bands self-hide, so they are never chrome.

**Exactly two toggles:** « ligne à vide » (**default off**) and « corde A–B » (**default on**).
Both live in the View Strip and both are persistable display preferences — voir § 8 — état, URL
et coquille applicative.

- **The unloaded line earns a toggle rather than permanence, because the prototype made its
  usefulness visibly scenario-dependent**: on Z1 (100 m, 4,4 m of sag) it shows what the rider
  does to the line, while at 1500 m it is indistinguishable from the loaded curve (1177 N
  against `H` ≈ 31 kN). Off by default, because the loaded case is the one the verdict judges.
- **The rider envelope stays metric — never drawn at a minimum pixel size.** At 1500 m the 2,1 m
  envelope is ~5 px tall. A non-metric glyph would misrepresent the one measurement the tool
  exists to produce; legibility is carried by the **printed** strip number, per ticket 02's rule
  that a printed read-out appears anywhere a number must be trusted rather than eyeballed.
- The cable polyline is regenerated per render from `sampleCable(state, n)` — **no polyline is
  ever stored in a result object** — voir § 2 — le contrat du moteur.

### The rider moves three ways, and there is no range input anywhere

| Path | Behaviour | Why it exists |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Scrub on the clearance strip** | pointer down anywhere on the strip SVG; snaps to sweep steps | its x axis *already is* rider position, which makes it a dimensionally honest scrub track |
| **Drag the rider marker on the profile** | snaps to sweep steps | the obvious affordance, and it is where the eye already is |
| **Typed `x` field in the View Strip** | **exact**, no snapping | precision, which neither pointer path can offer |

- **No `<input type="range">` exists anywhere in the app, because the clearance strip *is* the
  scrub track** — the one thing a slider in the Rail can never be, since a rail slider maps to
  nothing spatially. This is the reason the strip may never stop being a pointer surface: **when
  the Clearance Gate fails the strip keeps its axes and stays a scrub track**, `x` mapping to
  rider position whether or not clearance data exists (voir § 7 — validation et états d'erreur).
- **Typed positions get an exact §6.1-case-2 solve rather than being snapped and rewritten.**
  Outer bisection on `s_P` (monotone in `x`), inner 2×2 Newton; measured **~40 µs** (≈20
  bisection steps × ~2 µs warm-started). So the field reads back exactly what was typed and the
  drawn cable is the true state there. Snapping 99,7 → 101,2 and rewriting the field would break
  the map's « never discard user input silently » rule for the sake of one code path.
- **Pointer input snaps to precomputed sweep steps rather than solving, because at 1500 m 1 px ≈
  1,2 m — there is no precision to preserve.**
- **Densifying the grid until snapping is invisible was rejected**: sub-metre steps at 1500 m is
  ~3000 steps, pushing the sweep past 6 ms and the clearance pass past 20 ms — it would force
  the very Web Worker the measurements rule out (voir § 2 — le contrat du moteur).

#### Position reads as `x_D` in metres, with `%` as a quiet secondary

```
x [ 74,8 ] m · 50 %
```

- **Metres from A rather than a percentage alone, because it is the only unit that matches the
  chart's x axis, ticket 06's Stations, and how a line is actually paced out.** The percentage
  rides along in smaller type because « 748 m » is hard to place at a glance on a 1500 m span.
- **`s_D` never appears in the UI.** It stays the engine's material coordinate. B's prototyped
  triple read-out (`x = 99,7 m · 50 % · s_D = 101,5 m`) read as noise, and `s_D` drifts from
  `x_D` by ~0,5–2 % (fixture Z5: 60,15 against 59,96) — **exposing it only invites « pourquoi
  ces deux nombres diffèrent ? »**.
- Position round-trips in the URL as `x_D` **in metres**, not an index and not `s_D`, since the
  grid is derived from `(L₀, n)`. The « ×1 » peek is a transient and is never persisted.

#### `x_D` is a view parameter: it clamps on every path, and says so

`x_D` is a field with a row of its own, and it **clamps on every path** — geometry change, link
arrival and typed input — with a transient inline note rather than an error, because `x` selects
a *view*, and refusing to draw a valid span because the viewpoint is stale is worse than moving
the viewpoint: voir § 3 — le modèle de paramètres.

Under `Indéterminé` (coverage < 1) the scrub track has **gaps** where steps failed. Gaps are
drawn as an explicit discontinuity, **pointer input skips them**, and a typed `x` inside a gap
gets the exact §6.1-case-2 solve above — which may succeed where the swept step failed. Voir § 6
— sorties, verdict et provenance for `Indéterminé` itself.

### Clearance failure — four coordinated signals, two of them positional

This is the single thing the tool exists to reveal, so the signalling is deliberately redundant
— but **each signal answers a different question**.

| # | Signal | Question it answers | Positional? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | worded verdict naming `h_min` and its `x`, shown whenever the line fails **anywhere** | « cette conception échoue-t-elle ? » | no |
| 2 | red fill below zero on the clearance curve | « de combien, et où sur la traversée ? » | on the strip |
| 3 | tinted x-band on the profile, over **rider positions that violate** | « où le passager ne doit-il pas aller ? » | yes |
| 4 | cable and rider turn red **only while the rider is inside that band** | « suis-je dans la zone en ce moment ? » | yes |

- **Signal 3 covers the violating rider positions, not the x of the worst point.** The prototype
  had this backwards at first; the band answers « where must the rider not go? », which is a
  different set of x values. **Cable-red (signal 4) is reserved for « the rider is in the zone
  right now »**, which is what keeps « this design fails » and « you are standing in the failing
  zone » separable states.
- **A tint plus an edge, never a texture over the subject.** The heavy hatched full-height band
  first prototyped in B was rejected: it floods the chart and destroys legibility exactly when
  legibility matters most.

#### The two contact kinds are two separate verdicts

`evaluateClearance` already distinguishes them (`contact: "none" | "rider" | "cable"`) and the
UI must too, **because they are not the same class of claim**:

| Verdict | Claim | Provenance |
| --- | --- | --- |
| « Câble au sol » | the cable itself passes under the terrain | bare geometry — states itself flatly |
| « Enveloppe passager traversée » | true **only given `h_rider`** | **names the 2,1 m value, carries its « non confirmé » chip, and stays editable** |

That second row is the map's binding provenance rule applied to a **verdict** rather than a
coefficient — a new place for it. `docs/simulation-theory.md` §11 tags `h_rider = 2.1 m`
`[CONVENTION]` (`h_rider = l_lanyard + body-below-harness` — defaults `1.5 + 0.6 = 2.1 m`, §6.3)
with EN 15567's free-space/fall-space numbers `[PAYWALLED]`, so **a red banner that hid its
dependence would present a convention as a measurement.**

#### The clearance cell reports the worst clearance for the *current rider position*

§11's **inner** minimum: `h_clear(x; x_P) = z_cable(x; x_P) − z_ground(x) − h_rider`, minimised
over `x` at the current `x_P`. The chart draws its dimension line **wherever that minimum
actually sits**, so it visibly steps sideways onto a terrain bump the rider has already passed
(measured on ticket 07's failing case: rider at `x = 123,4 m`, governing point back at `x =
101 m`). The cell then relabels itself « Garde min (à x 101 m) ».

- **Under-the-rider-only was rejected: it can read positive while that same rider position is in
  violation a few metres away, which the aggregate `h_min` then contradicts.**
- The label changes **only** when the worst point is further than `max(1 m, 1 % of span)` from
  the rider, so the ordinary case reads plainly. (This is why the first-run rider ships at `x =
  75,0` m: 0,9 m from the traverse minimum, inside the rule, so the cell does not relabel itself
  on first paint.)

### Vertical exaggeration — the aspect identity, the band, and the ×1 floor

#### The identity, and what it retires

> **`VE = plot aspect × (x-domain span / z-domain span)`**, where *plot aspect* is
> `plot inner height / plot inner width`.

**Vertical exaggeration does not depend on pixel size at all** — only on the plot's aspect ratio
and the scenario's own flatness. The x domain is the shared `[d_A − 0,05 L, d_B + 0,05 L]`, i.e.
`1,1 L` wide; the z domain is the data span plus the plot's ±12 % margin.

**Tickets 02 and 07's published ×1,2…×4,7 is superseded.** It was never a property of the app —
it was a property of one box shape, 900×420 — and it is also ~10 % low, because ticket 06
widened the shared x domain to span + 5 % *after* ticket 07 measured on `[0, L]`. At the
prototype's own aspect the spec's domain gives **×1,57…×4,70**.

`k` = flatness = x-domain span / z-domain span, per scenario, both domain conventions:

| Scénario | `L` (m) | étendue z (m) | `k` sur `[0, L]` | `k` sur `1,1 L` (spec) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| z1 100 m plat | 100 | 8,0 | 10,081 | **11,089** |
| z3 120 m incliné | 120 | 28,8 | 3,360 | 3,696 |
| 50 m court | 50 | 9,0 | 4,481 | 4,929 |
| 1500 m long | 1500 | 214,5 | 5,640 | 6,204 |
| 1000 m quasi tendu | 1000 | 112,0 | 7,200 | 7,921 |
| 1000 m très mou | 1000 | 220,0 | 3,666 | 4,032 |
| 200 m en échec | 200 | 35,3 | 4,572 | 5,029 |
| *(mou profond — hors des sept)* | 200 | 182,5 | 0,884 | **0,972** |

And per aspect, across the seven scenarios:

| Aspect (H/W) | Boîte | VE sur `1,1 L` |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 4,507 | 390 px, rail **dans le flux** (69×311) | ×16,66 … ×49,98 |
| 0,4978 | 1440×900, **ancien ratio flex** (1119×557) | ×1,84 … **×5,52** |
| 0,50 | plafond de la bande | ×1,85 … ×5,54 |
| 0,4240 | défaut prototype 900×420 | ×1,57 … ×4,70 |
| **0,42** | **règle élue, aux trois viewports** | **×1,56 … ×4,67** |
| 0,35 | plancher de la bande | ×1,29 … ×3,88 |
| 0,1601 | paysage 844×330 (812×130) | ×0,59 … ×1,78 (**5/7 comprimés**) |

Two defects this convicts the prototype of, both from **one** mechanism — a chart dimension
derived from *window height*:

1. **The desktop was already out of budget.** `flex-[3]` / `flex-1` at 1440×900 gives the
   profile H/W 0,4978 ⇒ **VE ×5,02** on `[0, L]` (×5,52 on the spec domain), past ticket 07's
   verified ×4,7. Nobody had looked at a second window size.
2. **Auto-fit was unbounded below and nobody had looked.** A wide-short plot (844×330 landscape,
   H/W 0,1601) yields ×0,59…×1,78 with **five of seven scenarios vertically compressed**.

#### The published policy

```
profile plot height = 0.42 × W_inner              // rule (target aspect)
      Plot Aspect Band = [0.35, 0.50]             // guard: the layout may never leave it
strip outer height  = clamp(0.16 × W_inner, 96, 200)
      strip policy band = [72, 200]               // guard: the strip may never leave it
VE                  = max(autofit, 1)             // guard: never compress
```

**Published range: ×1 … ×5,5** — the floor, and the band ceiling's ×5,54 on the `1,1 L` domain.

Three rules, each with a guard that should never fire, and **all three guards written down,
because what broke here was a rule with no guard**.

- **The target is 0,42, not the prototype's 0,4240**, because the aspect that puts the flattest
  scenario exactly *on* ticket 07's ceiling is 0,4239 — 0,424 lands on ×4,70 with nothing left.
  0,42 keeps VE at ×1,56…×4,67, inside the verified ceiling by 0,03.
- **The band ceiling 0,50 is a legality bound on future layouts, not a target.** It permits
  ×5,54, which is why ×5,5 is what gets published.
- **The band floor 0,35 gives ×1,29…×3,88, so the aspect band alone guarantees `VE ≥ ×1` for all
  seven scenarios** (compression would need aspect < 0,2705). Box shape can no longer compress
  anything.
- **Compressing the sag in a clearance tool is the same class of error as exaggerating it, and
  it is worse — because it reads safe.** That is the whole reason the ×1 floor exists.

#### The ×1 floor is its own disclosed badge state

| État du badge | Quand | « ×1 vraie échelle » |
| --- | --- | --- |
| « Éch. verticale ×4,7 » | auto-fit ordinaire | actif |
| « Éch. verticale ×1 — vraie échelle » | clamp en vigueur, tout tient dans le cadre | **désactivé — déjà en vigueur** |
| « Éch. verticale ×1 — vraie échelle · câble hors cadre » | clamp en vigueur, la flèche sort du cadre | désactivé |

The badge is **chart furniture, not a footnote**, and is recomputed on every render.

The third row is reachable **from the data, not from the box**: a 200 m span at `L₀` 400 m (`w`
5,36 N/m, `EA` 6,90 MN, hauteurs d'ancrage 12 m, `h` −18 m) has a z span of 182,5 m ⇒ `k` =
0,972 ⇒ auto-fit asks for **×0,41 at every legal aspect**. Clamped to ×1 the plot shows ~87 m of
z in 144 px and the belly of the cable leaves the frame downward, **marked at the clip**.

- **Widening the x domain until ×1 fits is forbidden**: it would break the shared domain, i.e.
  the alignment mechanism the whole layout depends on.
- It is self-limiting: every case reachable inside the admissible envelope is already a
  ground-contact case (measured: garde −169,67 m, `contact: "cable"`, for exactly this line), so
  **the picture is never cut without the banner saying the line is on the ground.**

#### The peek control, not a selector

- **The user-facing VE selector is dead**, on evidence: forcing ×20 at 1500 m throws the cable
  out of the top of the frame and collapses the terrain into a triangle. **A manual selector is
  a loaded gun pointed at the chart's legibility.**
- What replaces it is **« ×1 vraie échelle »**, which lives in the **View Strip at all widths**
  (not the Rail: at 390 px the Rail is not co-visible with the chart, so ticket 07's « second
  disclosure channel beside the badge » would answer a question you cannot see the answer to).
- **Toggle under `@media (pointer: coarse)`; hold-to-peek on fine pointers.** Press-and-hold on
  touch collides with text selection, the context menu and scroll initiation, and ticket 07's
  gesture had no touch story at all. **Keyboard focus gets toggle semantics in both modes**
  (Space / Enter), since a held gesture has no keyboard equivalent.
- **Disabled whenever the ×1 clamp is already in force** (rows 2 and 3 above).

### One shared x scale object, one `GUTTER`, one inverse

```ts
/** Built once per (chart-column inline size, L, terrain source). Both SVGs consume THIS one. */
type SharedX = {
  width: number;             // chart-column inner width — the single ResizeObserver's inlineSize
  gutter: 24;                // GUTTER, left AND right, identical in both charts
  domain: [number, number];  // [-0.05*L, 1.05*L], clamped to the trace extent
  x(m: number): number;      // affine, domain -> [gutter, width - gutter]
  pxPerM: number;
  toMetres(clientX: number, rectLeft: number): number;  // THE inverse, one implementation
};
```

Three defects this retires, all measured on the only artifact that ever demonstrated the
pixel-alignment constraint:

| Defect | In the prototype | Cost |
| --- | --- | --- |
| two independently padded scales | profil `pad {left 16, right 16}` against strip `pad {left 24, right 24}` | the two x axes **coincide only at mid-span and diverge to 8 px at each end** — 8 px × 1500 m / 358 px = **34 m at a 1500 m span** on the narrow column, 10,7 m on the 1152 px desktop column |
| a hand-typed inverse, per call site | `(clientX − rect.left − pad.left) / pxPerM`, silently assuming `domain[0] === 0` | wrong by **`0,05 L` = 75 m at a 1500 m span** under the real domain |
| a `{900, 420}` observer seed | first client frame drew a 900 px chart in a 390 px box | see the `null` seed above |

- **`GUTTER = 24` on both sides of both charts, set by the strip**, whose clearance tick labels
  are drawn 6 px inside its left pad. **The profile pays 16 px of plot width for alignment** —
  4,7 % at 390 px, 1,4 % at desktop. Cheap at both ends.
- **The inverse is a method on the scale, not a formula to retype per call site.** Two pointer
  paths retyping it is how the `domain[0] === 0` assumption survived.
- The misalignment mattered **at the anchors** — precisely where the Handle Lane puts two drag
  handles. Voir § 4 — terrain et import CSV for the domain itself.

### The layout contract — every band height is a function of width

```ts
GUTTER         = 24                                 // both charts, both sides
PROFILE_CHROME = 28 /*badge*/ + 24 /*handle lane*/ + 24 /*x labels*/ = 76
PROFILE_ASPECT = 0.42                               // target; Plot Aspect Band [0.35, 0.50] guards it
STRIP_OUTER    = clamp(0.16 * W_inner, 96, 200)     // policy band [72, 200]
VIEW_STRIP     = 44                                 // one touch-height row
CELL_ROW       = 52 ; rows = colWidth >= 600 ? 1 : 2
BANNER         = content height: 44 (1 ligne) … 76 (3 lignes à 390 px)
RAIL_HEADER    = 44 ; MIN_RAIL = 120
```

> **Invariant: no chart dimension may read the window height.** Every band height is a function
> of the chart column's inner width. This is what fixes narrow *and* desktop with one rule, and
> it is also what makes the Skeleton correct at 390 px for free.

- **Aspect-derived height rather than a flex ratio, because `flex-[3]`/`flex-1` makes VE a
  function of window height** — which is *literally* how ×5,02 happened. One rule makes the
  narrow case a special case rather than an exception, and it reproduces the prototype's desktop
  proportions to within 2 px.
- The strip's floor of **96 px sits 24 px above the policy floor of 72 px, deliberately, because
  96 px is a touch lane**: 96 − 30 px of chrome = 66 px of drawn curve, and the whole 96 px SVG
  is the hit surface — over twice the 44/48 px minimum. Measured, **the strip is the best touch
  target in the app, not the worst.**
- At narrow the strip drops to 2 clearance ticks plus the zero line and its caption shortens
  **at the same B1 threshold, so no new breakpoint**: « Garde au sol (m) selon la position du
  passager — glisser pour déplacer » above 600 px, « Garde au sol (m) — glisser » below. The
  prototype's caption naming `h_clear` goes: French UI, identifiers in code.

#### The three pixel budgets, each summing to the viewport

| Bande | A · 390×664 (`svh`, B1+B2) | B · 768×1024 (B1 only) | C · 1440×900 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| colonne graphique | 390 (pleine largeur) | 480 | 1152 |
| `W_inner` = col − 48 | 342 | 432 | 1104 |
| créneau bandeau | 76 (3 lignes) | 60 (2 lignes) | 44 (1 ligne) |
| profil = 28 + tracé + 24 + 24 | 220 (tracé **144**) | 257 (tracé **181**) | 540 (tracé **464**) |
| aspect obtenu ⇒ VE | 0,4211 ⇒ ×1,56…×4,67 | 0,4190 ⇒ ×1,55…×4,65 | 0,4203 ⇒ ×1,56…**×4,66** |
| bande de garde | 96 (plancher) | 96 (plancher) | 177 |
| View Strip | 44 | 44 | 44 |
| cellules | 104 (3 col × 2 rangées) | 104 | 52 (5 en une rangée) |
| **bloc** | **540** | **561** | **857** |
| reste | rail en flux **124** | marge de boîtage **463** | marge de boîtage **43** |
| **total** | **664** | **1024** | **900** |

```
A · 390 × 664 — ordre vertical, rail au repos
┌ (conditionnel) bandeau « clés ignorées », pleine largeur, rejetable ··  0 | 60 ┐
├ CHART BLOCK ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ créneau bandeau  (verdict | invite régime | erreurs bloquantes | refus)   76   │
│ profil    28 badge + 144 tracé + 24 couloir de poignées + 24 axe x  =   220    │
│ bande de garde au sol  (= la piste de scrub)                              96   │
│ VIEW STRIP   [à vide] [corde]        [×1]     x [ 74,8 ] m · 50 %         44   │
│ cellules — grille 3 col × 2 rangées, « Garde min » sur 2 colonnes        104   │
├ RAIL (en flux, order-last, pleine largeur, scroller propre) ─── 540 ───────────┤
│ en-tête du rail  (nom · unité de force · Copier le lien · ⋯)              44   │
│ « Paramètres » + premier champ                                            80   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 664 ───────────┘
```

Those **124 px of resting rail** clear `MIN_RAIL = 120` by 4 px, which is why rejecting an app
bar in `__root.tsx` is arithmetic and not taste: a 44 px title bar — `RAIL_HEADER`'s own height
— would be spent straight out of the form. With the ignored-keys strip up the rail drops to
64 px — tolerable only because that strip is dismissible and the block collapses on the first
scroll.

**The letterbox margin is deliberate, quantified, and left empty**: **43 px** at the reference
desktop and **463 px** on a 768×1024 tablet in portrait — the two budgets in the table above. It
is a residual that varies with viewport height and is **never** redistributed.

- **Letterbox rather than fill, because both ways of filling it are the defect this section
  exists to fix**: growing the profile is *literally* how ×5,02 happened, and pushing the growth
  into the strip runs it to its 200 px ceiling on every desktop — the over-feeding that ceiling
  exists to bound (absorption produces a 665 px tail on a 1024×1366 portrait tablet). **A
  height-fed band is precisely the mechanism both measured defects came from.**
- The shell is **fixed-height in both modes** (`dvh`, `overflow: hidden` on the body) with
  internal scrollers. **A document scroll was rejected**: the collapse trigger would then have
  to read `window.scrollY` *and* survive iOS's document-scroll-under-keyboard behaviour, where a
  fixed shell plus `interactive-widget=resizes-content` gives the same result with one known
  scroller.

#### Two breakpoints, stated as what fails

| | Ce qui casse | Seuil | Mécanisme |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **B1** | la rangée de cellules ne tient plus cinq valeurs | largeur de la **colonne graphique** < 600 px | `@container` (inline-size) |
| **B2** | le rail fixe accapare plus d'un tiers de la largeur : la colonne du graphe tombe sous 480 px | **viewport** < 768 px | media query, reflux en une colonne |

- **B1 is a container query, not a viewport query, because the 288 px rail means the chart
  column is never the viewport.** Tailwind 4.3.3 ships the `@container` variant, so it costs no
  dependency and no config file.
- **B2's threshold is the narrowest width at which the two-column layout still budgets.** At
  768 px of viewport the chart column is 480 px and `W_inner` 432 px — budget B above, which
  sums to the viewport. Below it the rail's fixed 288 px eats the column faster than the span
  can shrink. **What no longer justifies B2:** ticket 15 measured aspect 0,91–1,25 and VE
  ×3,1…×12,6 at a 479 px column, but that was under the **rejected** `flex-[3]`/`flex-1`
  mechanism. Under the elected width-derived rule the aspect is 0,42 at every width by
  construction, so what breaks below 768 px is the plot's **width**, not its shape.
- **The one number everything is downstream of**: the rail is `w-72 shrink-0` = **288 px, not
  280**, so at a 390 px viewport the chart column is **101 px** and the plot's x range is `101 −
  16 − 16` = **69 px** on the prototype's padding — **53 px** under the elected `GUTTER = 24`,
  i.e. worse. The whole span in 69 px, aspect 4,507, VE ×16,7…×50,0, all three 36 px drag
  targets overlapping on a 69 px track. Every other narrow-screen symptom is downstream of that.
- **No third breakpoint.** The ~450 px threshold one might expect — where the drag targets cover
  the whole track — needs no rule, because B2 has already restored a full-width track at 390 px.
- **One viewport breakpoint at 900 px collapsing B1 into B2 was rejected**: it clips five values
  in a 1000 px window that still believes it is a desktop, and nobody tests 1000 px.

#### The Rail at narrow: an in-flow region, CSS only

The flex row becomes `flex-col`; the `aside` keeps `overflow-y-auto` and gains `order-last` plus
full width. **No bottom sheet, no tabs, no second route, and no `useMediaQuery` branch anywhere
in the layout.**

- **The sheet was declined on occlusion, not availability.** Base UI 1.6.0 *does* ship a Drawer
  with `modal={false}`, snap points and swipe, so it costs no new dependency — but **a portalled
  fixed popup covers the bottom of the viewport at every snap point, and that is exactly where
  the five read-out cells and the strip's zero crossing live.** It is the same occlusion that
  killed A's HUD and C's tooltip. An in-flow region pushes instead of covering.
- **No `useMediaQuery`, second and independent reason**: it returns `defaultMatches` on the
  server and on first mount, so a JS-branched tree paints the desktop layout and then
  re-measures — a visible 900→390 chart jump on every narrow first load.

#### The Chart Block collapses to a Pinned Safety Header

No toggle, no new control. The block is whole while the Rail sits at `scrollTop === 0`; it
collapses when **(a)** the Rail scrolls, **(b)** a Rail field takes focus, or **(c)** it cannot
fit — `blockHeight > availableHeight − MIN_RAIL`. Reversible by scrolling the Rail back to top
or tapping the header (chevron, `aria-label` « Afficher le profil »).

| État | Contenu | Hauteur |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **whole** | tout le bloc | 540 |
| **pinned** | créneau bandeau + une cellule vive « Garde min » | 76 + 44 = 120 |
| **pinned + x** | idem, plus la View Strip, tant que le champ `x` a le focus | 164 |
| **compact** | une seule rangée : bandeau sur une ligne, « Garde min » en ligne | 44 |

- Arithmetic forces (a) and (b): at 390×844 the portrait `svh` is ~664 px and the soft keyboard
  takes ~336 px, leaving ~328 px — **a 540 px block cannot coexist with a focused field**. And
  the Rail is not a thing that shrinks: five parameter groups plus four output sections is ~60
  rows, unusable through a 124 px window.
- `compact` fires when `--kb > 0` or available height < 420 px, which is what makes the keyboard
  case work: 664 − 336 − 44 = **284 px of usable form**.
- **`pinned + x` exists because the View Strip lives *inside* the Chart Block**: without it,
  focusing the `x` field would collapse away the very field being typed into. With it the user
  types a position and watches « Garde min » move with the keyboard open — the best argument
  available for putting `x` in the View Strip.
- **Unmissability survives the collapse** because signals 1 and the printed « Garde min » number
  are the two **non-positional** signals — the two that do not need the plot.
- **The pinned header's banner slot carries whichever occupant precedence gives it, never only a
  verdict**: a blocking-error summary with its jump link, one of the three refusal states,
  « Saisie en cours », the verdict, or the no-regime prompt. Voir § 7 — validation et états
  d'erreur. A **blocking error force-opens and pins its Rail section** at narrow widths, so its
  Fix Action is reachable in every layout.
- **Not persisted.** The state is derived from scroll position, focus and available height, so a
  stored value would fight all three of its own inputs.

#### The View Strip, and its one abbreviation rule

```
[✓ ligne à vide]  [✓ corde A–B]          [ ×1 vraie échelle ]     x [ 74,8 ] m · 50 %
```

At 342 px of inner width and `text-[11px]`, full labels need 93 + 76 + 111 + 128 + 24 of gaps =
**432 px**, which does not fit. **Below 600 px of column** (the same B1 threshold — no new
breakpoint) the toggles read « à vide » and « corde » and the peek control reads « ×1 », each
keeping its full string as `aria-label` and `title`: 59 + 48 + 44 + 128 + 24 = **303 px**,
inside
342. The row is 44 px tall, so every control in it is a legal touch target.

- **Abbreviating rather than wrapping to two rows**: 44 px out of `MIN_RAIL`, the tightest
  budget in the app, to avoid abbreviating two layer names.
- **The `x` field and « ×1 » live here rather than in the Rail** (amending ticket 07 §9 and
  ticket 09 §7's rail placement of the toggles), **because at 390 px the Rail is not co-visible
  with the chart** — the block collapses precisely when the Rail comes into view — so a
  rail-only `x` puts the app's only precision input path out of sight of the thing it moves.

#### The read-out cells wrap to three columns; « Garde min » spans two

```
≥ 600 px                                        < 600 px
┌────────┬────────┬────────┬────────┬────────┐  ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐
│ Garde  │ T A    │ T B    │ Pente  │ Flèche │  │ T anc. A │ T anc. B │ Pente    │
│ 1,24 m │612 daN │604 daN │ −3,4 % │13,78 m │  ├──────────┴──────────┼──────────┤
└────────┴────────┴────────┴────────┴────────┘  │ Garde min (à x 1010 m) │ Flèche │
                       ↑ « à la position du     │  1,24 m                │13,78 m │
                         passager »             └────────────────────────┴────────┘
```

Exactly two rows, 104 px, **all five cells kept — no subset, no horizontal scroll.**

- **The value is the binding constraint, not the label**: `font-mono text-lg` needs ~97 px for a
  nine-character value (« 12345 daN ») plus 24 px of `px-3`, so five cells need ≈605 px — hence
  600. A 3-column grid at 390 px gives 130 px per cell, 106 px of content. **Wrapping a label is
  benign; clipping a value is not.**
- **Horizontal scroll is out because it hides Garde min behind a gesture that competes with the
  scrub.** A subset re-litigates ticket 07 §9 and would delete the printed number that justified
  drawing a sub-pixel rider envelope at 1500 m in the first place. **Garde min and its position
  qualifier are non-negotiable at every width.**
- The row carries its own scope label, **« à la position du passager »**, because at narrow it
  ends up directly against the Rail's `Résultats` (« sur toute la traversée ») — measured on the
  first-run screen, which prints **2,49 m in the strip against 2,44 m in the Rail** from the
  very first paint.
- **`Pente locale` reads in percent, downhill negative**, at 1 decimal — « −3,4 % » here,
  « −11,1 % » on the first-run line. Fixed here because no earlier ticket fixed that cell's unit
  or sign while the precision table and the first-run record both quote it: percent matches the
  `flèche / portée` read-out and an x axis in metres, and the sign follows
  `docs/simulation-theory.md` §2's frame, where `h < 0` means B below A. **Degrees were
  rejected**, because a degree figure invites reading the chart's *drawn* angle, which the
  vertical exaggeration has already distorted.
- **The cells span the full column width, not the gutter-inset plot width** — they are cells,
  not plot content, and aligning them to the plot would imply a positional correspondence that
  does not exist.
- **Under « Aucun résultat » the row is replaced by one line spanning it, keeping the row's
  height** — the ~104 px band does not collapse and nothing else on the page jumps. **Not « — »
  per cell**: that glyph belongs to `Indéterminé` alone. Voir § 7 — validation et états
  d'erreur.

#### The Handle Lane, reserved in both modes

24 px between the bottom of the plot area and the x-axis labels, **inside the profile SVG, at
every width and in both terrain modes**:

```
│   … aire de tracé (144 px à 390) …                    │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ ← bas de l'aire de tracé
│   ├──A ═══════════════════════════════════ B──┤       │  couloir de poignées  24 px
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│   0        250        500        750       1000 m     │  étiquettes axe x     24 px
```

The rider stays on the cable; **the two anchors move down here**, each with a **44 × 48 px** hit
target spanning the lane plus the label band — **entirely below the plot area**, so confusing
one with the rider is geometrically impossible rather than merely unlikely.

- **The collision is structural, not a sizing accident.** The anchors are pinned at the domain
  edges, so at 390 px a 44 px anchor target covers the first and last 12 % of the span, and the
  rider glyph overlaps an anchor handle whenever `x_D` is within ~46 m of an anchor at `L =
  1000`. **You cannot size your way out of two targets that are always at `x = 0` and `x = L`.**
  Putting handles outside the plot area is ticket 07 §1's own rule, and the lane also retires
  the axis-label collision ticket 06 named.
- **Reserved in both modes, so the profile's height never changes on import** — and not blank in
  manual mode: the lane draws the **span bracket** `├──A ═══ B──┤`, non-interactive, with ticks
  at 0 and `L`. On import the same bracket's ends become the draggable Station handles. That
  gives the reserved 24 px a job in manual mode — marking the span boundary inside the 5 %
  margin, which is the « span markers » that must keep drawing when the Clearance Gate fails.

#### Touch, pointer and keyboard

**Hit radii, CSS only, no JS branch.** The rider's grab circle becomes a transparent circle with
`pointer-events: all`, `r = 11`, and a transparent **stroke**: `stroke-width: 14px` ⇒ 36 px (`r
+ sw/2 = 18`), `stroke-width: 22px` ⇒ **44 px under `@media (pointer: coarse)`**. Stroke width
is used deliberately in place of the SVG2 geometry property `r`, **whose CSS support is uneven**
— a stroke is a plain presentation property everywhere, so the whole rule is one media query.

| Surface | `touch-action` | Raison |
| --- | --- | --- |
| profil SVG (couloir de poignées compris) | `pan-y` | glisser en x = déplacer ; glisser en y = défiler |
| bande de garde (piste de scrub) | `pan-y` | idem ; c'est le chemin pointeur obligatoire |
| view strip, cellules, rail | défaut | rien à capturer |

- **`pan-y` rather than `touch-none`**: `touch-none` blocks vertical page scroll across ~70 % of
  the viewport — harmless in the prototype's fixed shell, **load-bearing the moment the layout
  scrolls**. On the Handle Lane it is exactly right: an anchor only ever moves in x, so a
  vertical drag starting on a handle should scroll the page rather than do nothing.

**Keyboard, four pieces** (the viewport meta is the app shell's to write — voir § 8 — état, URL
et coquille applicative):

1. `interactive-widget=resizes-content` on the viewport meta. Without it the default
   `resizes-visual` applies: the layout viewport, and therefore `dvh`, does not shrink when the
   keyboard opens, so **the keyboard covers the bottom ~336 px of a 664 px viewport — exactly
   where the reflow puts the Rail.**
2. **A `visualViewport` `resize` + `scroll` listener** writing `--kb` (`layoutHeight −
   visualViewport.height − offsetTop`) onto `documentElement`. **Unconditional and
   self-neutralising**: where the meta tag is honoured `--kb` computes to 0 and costs nothing;
   on older iOS Safari, which ignores the tag, it is what drives the `compact` state and the
   Rail's bottom padding. **There is no feature test for the meta tag, so a conditional listener
   is not on offer.**
3. `scrollIntoView({ block: 'center' })` on field focus, inside the Rail's own scroller —
   `behavior` left at `auto`, **because a smooth scroll races the keyboard animation**.
4. Fields are `<input type="text" inputMode="decimal">`, plus `enterKeyHint="done"`, and Enter
   commits and blurs — **on iOS there is otherwise no way to dismiss the keyboard** without
   hunting for a key that may not exist.

This is the one thing the rejected Drawer would have given for free
(`Drawer.VirtualKeyboardProvider` exists precisely for it), so **declining the sheet obliged
stating the replacement.**

#### Portrait is the supported posture, and the app says nothing about it

**Landscape is neither privileged nor blocked: no rotate prompt, no orientation lock, no hint.**

The measurement that justifies refusing one: on a 390×844 phone, landscape `svh` is ~330 px,
while the Chart Block at 844 px of width budgets `44 + (28 + 334 + 24 + 24) + 127 + 44 + 52` =
**677 px** — so collapse condition (c) fires and **landscape always opens collapsed**, showing
the Pinned Safety Header above the form. The alternative, squashing the profile into the height
available, is exactly the aspect-0,1601 case measured at **×0,59…×1,78 with five of seven
scenarios compressed** — the failure the ×1 clamp exists to refuse. And the keyboard in
landscape leaves ~150 px, i.e. ~106 px of Rail under the compact header, so **the form is
unusable in the posture too**.

- **A rotate prompt would recommend the one configuration in which the app has neither its
  picture nor its form — it would be advertising its own worst state.**
- The intuition behind the question — a zip-line profile is intrinsically wide, so give it a
  wide screen — is answered instead by **letterboxing a wide box inside a tall viewport**,
  because **the app is not a chart**: it is a chart plus a banner plus five cells plus a ~60-row
  form, and three of those four are vertical costs.
- Landscape needing no rule is a *consequence* of the collapse trigger, not a special case —
  which is the same property that makes one aspect rule cover 390 px and 1440 px.

### Re-checks owed in the real build

Not risks — numbers computed from the prototype's Tailwind classes rather than rendered, to be
confirmed once the real layout exists (and before the prototype is deleted):

- **The banner heights 44 / 60 / 76 px** at the three budgets.
- **The View Strip's label widths** (the 432 px / 303 px arithmetic above).
- **The three pixel budgets** at 390×664, 768×1024 and 1440×900, each summing to the viewport.
- The first-run screen's auto-fit VE, measured at **≈ ×1,5** on a 1080×420 plot — the first
  concrete point on the band the app actually ships, inside the Plot Aspect Band and clear of
  the ×1 floor.


---

## § 6 — Sorties, verdict de sécurité et provenance

Source : [ticket 08](./issues/08-results-panel-and-provenance-warnings.md) et ses cinq
amendements datés du 2026-08-12 (dénominateur de couverture, libellé de la Fix Action, trous
dans la piste de scrub, palier de l'avertissement terrain, copie de la divulgation terrain
manuel), plus le dénominateur WorkSafe fixé par [ticket
